```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We evaluate depth fingerprinting along four axes: cross-generator classification (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}), differential depth response of individual features (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}), comparison with existing detection baselines (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), and downstream utility for data curation (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
5	
6	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
7	\label{sec:results-classification}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} reports leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) macro-F1 for 3-class depth estimation (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) across six generators spanning three model families and parameter counts from 0.6B to 8.2B.
10	
11	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.789 (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}). Because gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), the gen-0 class F1 (0.94) may be inflated; we therefore report a leak-free binary evaluation (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, F1\,=\,0.723) as the primary metric.
12	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.641 (Qwen3-8B) to 0.795 (Pythia-1B); 3-class F1 ranges from 0.683 to 0.856.
13	Per-class F1 varies by depth: gen-0\,=\,0.94, gen-2+\,=\,0.80, gen-1\,=\,0.64; the primary difficulty lies in distinguishing gen-1 from gen-2+, where feature margins narrow.
14	
15	\paragraph{Cross-scale transfer.}
16	The six-generator evaluation includes Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters), confirming that depth signal persists beyond the $\leq$2.8B training generators, though with a moderate gap: Qwen3-8B binary F1\,=\,0.641 (CI [0.625, 0.657]) vs.\ 0.68--0.80 for smaller generators.
17	Adding Qwen3-8B data improves classification on the original five generators (+0.8\,pp to 0.810); a volume-matched control confirms 94\% of this gain is attributable to generator diversity rather than data quantity.
18	
19	\paragraph{Cross-family generalization.}
20	Leave-one-family-out (LOFO) evaluation---holding out an entire model family and training on the remaining two---yields macro-F1 of 0.772 (Pythia held out), 0.762 (OLMo held out), and 0.754 (Qwen held out), confirming that depth features transfer across architectural families with only modest degradation (average $\Delta$\,=\,$-$2.6\,pp vs.\ LOGO).
21	
22	\paragraph{Cross-model chains.}
23	Cross-model chains---where each generation step uses a randomly selected generator---yield 3-class F1\,=\,0.809 and binary F1\,=\,0.764 under 5-fold stratified cross-validation, confirming that depth signal persists under generator heterogeneity.
24	
25	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
26	Text length correlates weakly with depth (Spearman $|\rho| < 0.14$).
27	Length-normalized feature trends are preserved, and length-matched subsampling yields comparable or higher F1 ($\Delta$\,=\,+0.041), ruling out length as a confound (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
28	
29	
30	\begin{table}[t]
31	\centering
32	\small
33	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
34	\toprule
35	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
36	\midrule
37	Pythia-1B       & Pythia & 1.0B & 0.856 & \textbf{0.795} & [0.780, 0.809] \\
38	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia & 2.8B & 0.804 & \textbf{0.723} & [0.709, 0.739] \\
39	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo   & 1.5B & 0.764 & \textbf{0.677} & [0.660, 0.692] \\
40	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen   & 0.6B & 0.824 & \textbf{0.756} & [0.742, 0.772] \\
41	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen   & 1.7B & 0.806 & \textbf{0.748} & [0.732, 0.762] \\
42	Qwen3-8B       & Qwen   & 8.2B & 0.683 & \textbf{0.641} & [0.625, 0.657] \\
43	\midrule
44	\multicolumn{3}{l}{Average (6-gen LOGO)} & 0.789 & \textbf{0.723} & --- \\
45	\multicolumn{3}{l}{5-gen LOGO (excl.\ Qwen3-8B)} & 0.802 & \textbf{0.740} & --- \\
46	\bottomrule
47	\end{tabular}
48	\caption{6-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Gen-0 samples are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); 3-class results are supplementary. Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric.}
49	\label{tab:logo-results}
50	\end{table}
51	
52	\paragraph{Ordinal regression comparison.}
53	CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral_ordinal} and Frank-Hall ordinal reduction \cite{frank_hall_ordinal} achieve 3-class F1 of 0.771 and 0.793 respectively, with all CIs overlapping the standard XGBoost (0.789).
54	Explicitly encoding ordinal structure does not yield a measurable gain, likely because 3-class granularity provides insufficient ordinal resolution for ranking-aware losses.
55	
56	
57	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
58	\label{sec:results-differential}
59	
60	Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.
61	This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution---the text becomes progressively more predictable (mean shifts), while the functional form of the distribution is preserved (shape is invariant).
62	
63	\begin{figure}[t]
64	\centering
65	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
66	\caption{Differential depth response: Spearman correlations between individual features and generation depth. Central-tendency and diversity features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show strong monotonic drift, while shape features (entropy std, tail decay) remain near-stationary.}
67	\label{fig:feature-drift}
68	\end{figure}
69	
70	Individual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
71	drifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.475$),
72	while shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\rho| = 0.089$).
73	
74	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
75	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.
76	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.789) significantly outperforms both surface-only features (TTR, hapax\_ratio; F1\,=\,0.729) and reference-only features (entropy mean/std/max/min, tail\_decay; F1\,=\,0.745; McNemar $p \approx 0$).
77	Permutation importance ranks hapax ratio highest (0.269), followed by entropy mean (0.164) and TTR (0.159).
78	Surface features capture vocabulary impoverishment; reference features capture distributional shift---neither subsumes the other.
79	
80	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
81	To confirm that differential depth response is not an artifact of the Pythia-410M reference model, we recompute all features using two cross-family references: OLMo-2-1B (1.5B) and Qwen3-0.6B.
82	All three reference models achieve comparable LOGO F1: Pythia-410M\,=\,0.803, OLMo-2-1B\,=\,0.807, Qwen3-0.6B\,=\,0.816 (spread 0.013; Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}).
83	The Qwen3-0.6B reference shares a model family with three of the six generators, introducing potential self-referencing bias; however, its +1.3\,pp advantage over the non-self Pythia-410M baseline suggests any such bias is limited.
84	Entropy drift direction is consistently negative across all reference models (Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}), confirming that the differential depth response is a property of recursive generation, not of the specific reference model used.
85	
86	\input{table4}
87	
88	
89	\begin{table}[t]
90	\centering
91	\small
92	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
93	\toprule
94	\textbf{Feature set} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & \textbf{Top feature (importance)} \\
95	\midrule
96	Full (7 features)     & 0.789 & hapax\_ratio (0.269) \\
97	Surface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\_ratio (0.320) \\
98	Reference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\_mean (0.218) \\
99	\bottomrule
100	\end{tabular}
101	\caption{Feature ablation (6-gen LOGO, 3-class). Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features.}
102	\label{tab:ablation}
103	\end{table}
104	
105	Leave-one-feature-out analysis confirms that 6 of 7 features contribute positively, with entropy\_max showing the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
106	
107	
108	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
109	\label{sec:results-baselines}
110	
111	\begin{figure}[t]
112	\centering
113	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.png}
114	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals for each detection baseline and the 7-feature ensemble. Per-generator held-out F1 varies more for single detectors than for the ensemble.}
115	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
116	\end{figure}
117	
118	We repurpose four existing detection scores as ordinal depth predictors by training the same classifier on each score as a single feature, using identical LOGO evaluation.
119	Because gen-0 seed texts are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), 3-class LOGO evaluation creates a train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
120	We therefore report both 3-class and binary (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) results; the binary metric is the primary measure of depth discrimination (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
121	
122	\begin{table}[t]
123	\centering
124	\small
125	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
126	\toprule
127	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{Per-gen std} \\
128	\midrule
129	7-feature ensemble    & 0.789 & \textbf{0.723} & 0.051 \\
130	4-det.\ + surface     & \textbf{0.805} & 0.724 & 0.049 \\
131	4-detector ens.\      & 0.796 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\
132	Avg-LogProb           & 0.737 & 0.712 & 0.044 \\
133	LD-Score              & 0.655 & 0.701 & 0.045 \\
134	FastDetectGPT         & 0.688 & 0.616 & 0.056 \\
135	Binoculars            & 0.417 & 0.524 & 0.022 \\
136	\bottomrule
137	\end{tabular}
138	\caption{Depth classification under unified 6-gen LOGO evaluation. \textbf{3-class}: gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ (gen-0 samples shared across generators; see \S\ref{sec:generation}). \textbf{Binary}: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ only, eliminating the gen-0 train--test overlap. Per-gen std measures cross-generator consistency of the binary metric. The 7-feature ensemble (0.723) matches 4-det.\ + surface (0.724) on binary F1; its contribution is simplicity---7 handcrafted features vs.\ 4 external detectors plus surface features.}
139	\label{tab:baselines}
140	\end{table}
141	
142	The 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723, outperforming every individual baseline ($p < 0.001$ for all; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
143	Combining all four detection scores into a 4-detector ensemble yields binary F1\,=\,0.712---below the 7-feature ensemble; adding surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) raises it to 0.724, matching the 7-feature ensemble and confirming that surface diversity features capture signal complementary to detection scores.
144	The multi-detector 3-class advantage (0.796 vs.\ 0.789) is driven entirely by gen-0 classification; existing detectors do not capture the finer depth signal between gen-1 and gen-2+.
145	
146	
147	\subsection{Downstream Utility}
148	\label{sec:results-downstream}
149	
150	Depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality.
151	Fine-tuning OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) shows that data quantity dominates depth composition: random subsampling (ppl\,=\,2.367, 95\% CI [2.367, 2.367]) outperforms depth-stratified selection (ppl\,=\,2.387, 95\% CI [2.387, 2.388]) at equal sample count; $\Delta$\,=\,0.020, $p$\,=\,0.25 by paired permutation test ($n$\,=\,3 seeds; minimum achievable $p$ for this sample size).
152	Cross-seed variance is below 0.001 for all variants, so the ranking is stable but the test is underpowered (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}).
153
```

_source line 929 · 2026-05-16T05:16:13.015Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JB36uuRGMrMx1a6QpTUrch`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-crawled corpora increasingly contain LLM-generated text---estimates suggest that 7--17\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and this proportion grows as generated text re-enters the web and seeds subsequent generation.
5	The result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text.
6	Understanding the distributional properties of such recursive generation is a prerequisite for characterizing text provenance, yet the phenomenon itself---how distributional features evolve across generation depths---remains underexplored.
7	
8	We study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic generation chains under controlled conditions, using six base (pretrained) models from three families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen) spanning multiple scales.
9	We restrict to base models as a deliberate design choice: instruction-tuning introduces confounds---output length variability, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping---that obscure the generation mechanism's intrinsic behavior.
10	Preliminary experiments confirm this concern: instruct models show substantially degraded depth signal (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), motivating the controlled base-model setting as the appropriate starting point for isolating recursive drift phenomena.
11	
12	Existing detectors \cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt}, like classical out-of-distribution methods \cite{ood_baseline,energy_ood,mahalanobis_ood}, provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, conflating qualitatively different material.
13	A first-generation continuation retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties; deeper recursive text exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity and compressed perplexity.
14	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) rather than producing a binary label---and refer to the overall framework as \emph{depth fingerprinting}.
15	No existing method addresses this task in non-paraphrastic continuation chains.
16	When repurposed as ordinal depth predictors, individual detection scores exhibit high cross-generator variance (binary F1 from 0.52 to 0.71; \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), making any single detector unreliable.
17	Concurrent work on iterative paraphrasing chains \cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains} analyzes convergence under semantic-preservation constraints; we study non-paraphrastic chains where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal.
18	
19	We characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy, type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.
20	We hypothesize that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form; under this account, the sampling mechanism governs distributional shape regardless of input content.
21	The combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+).
22	However, the strength of this signal is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains exhibit stronger drift than elaboration or rewriting chains (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
23	
24	We additionally report a negative finding: despite the effectiveness of quality-based data curation for pretraining \cite{fineweb,dolma}, depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling at equal sample count (perplexity: 2.367 vs.\ 2.387).
25	
26	Our contributions are:
27	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
28	    \item \textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for characterizing how distributional properties evolve across recursive generation depths.
29	    \item \textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across six base-model generators spanning three model families: a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination, outperforming all individual detection baselines ($p < 0.001$).
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
32	The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
33	\S\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on text detection, model collapse, and data quality.
34	\S\ref{sec:method} describes generation chain construction, feature extraction, and evaluation protocol.
35	\S\ref{sec:results} presents classification results, differential depth response analysis, and baseline comparisons.
36	\S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses mechanistic hypotheses and scope boundaries.
37	
```

</details>

```json
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWeb-crawled corpora increasingly contain LLM-generated text---estimates suggest that 7--17\\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and this proportion grows as generated text re-enters the web and seeds subsequent generation.\nThe result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text.\nUnderstanding the distributional properties of such recursive generation is a prerequisite for characterizing text provenance, yet the phenomenon itself---how distributional features evolve across generation depths---remains underexplored.\n\nWe study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic generation chains under controlled conditions, using six base (pretrained) models from three families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen) spanning multiple scales.\nWe restrict to base models as a deliberate design choice: instruction-tuning introduces confounds---output length variability, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping---that obscure the generation mechanism's intrinsic behavior.\nPreliminary experiments confirm this concern: instruct models show substantially degraded depth signal (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), motivating the controlled base-model setting as the appropriate starting point for isolating recursive drift phenomena.\n\nExisting detectors \\cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt}, like classical out-of-distribution methods \\cite{ood_baseline,energy_ood,mahalanobis_ood}, provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, conflating qualitatively different material.\nA first-generation continuation retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties; deeper recursive text exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity and compressed perplexity.\nWe formulate \\emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+) rather than producing a binary label---and refer to the overall framework as \\emph{depth fingerprinting}.\nNo existing method addresses this task in non-paraphrastic continuation chains.\nWhen repurposed as ordinal depth predictors, individual detection scores exhibit high cross-generator variance (binary F1 from 0.52 to 0.71; \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}), making any single detector unreliable.\nConcurrent work on iterative paraphrasing chains \\cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains} analyzes convergence under semantic-preservation constraints; we study non-paraphrastic chains where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal.\n\nWe characterize a \\emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy, type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.\nWe hypothesize that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form; under this account, the sampling mechanism governs distributional shape regardless of input content.\nThe combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves binary F1\\,=\\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+).\nHowever, the strength of this signal is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains exhibit stronger drift than elaboration or rewriting chains (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nWe additionally report a negative finding: despite the effectiveness of quality-based data curation for pretraining \\cite{fineweb,dolma}, depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling at equal sample count (perplexity: 2.367 vs.\\ 2.387).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for characterizing how distributional properties evolve across recursive generation depths.\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across six base-model generators spanning three model families: a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves binary F1\\,=\\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination, outperforming all individual detection baselines ($p < 0.001$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on text detection, model collapse, and data quality.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} describes generation chain construction, feature extraction, and evaluation protocol.\n\\S\\ref{sec:results} presents classification results, differential depth response analysis, and baseline comparisons.\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses mechanistic hypotheses and scope boundaries.\n",
      "numLines": 37,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 37
    }
  },
```

```text
1	Can depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?
2	We fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\ref{tab:downstream}).
3	
4	\begin{table}[h]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\begin{tabular}{lrc}
8	\toprule
9	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{Test PPL} \\
10	\midrule
11	All depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
12	Size-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
13	Depth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
14	Binary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\pm$ 0.000 \\
15	Depth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\pm$ 0.001 \\
16	\bottomrule
17	\end{tabular}
18	\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}
19	\label{tab:downstream}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	The results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.
23	Training on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).
24	At equal sample count ($n$\,=\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367) \emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387) and binary detection filtering (2.389).
25	Cross-seed variance is below 0.002 across all variants, confirming the robustness of this null result.
26	
27	Restricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\,=\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.
28	
29	Bootstrap analysis (10{,}000 resamples over 3 seeds) yields a 95\% CI for the ppl difference between size-matched and depth 0--1 of [0.019, 0.020], with permutation-test $p = 0.25$ (exact; $n$\,=\,3 limits resolution to $p_{\min}$\,=\,0.25) and Cohen's $d = 34.8$.
30	The analogous comparison with binary-filtered gives CI [0.021, 0.023], $p = 0.25$, $d = 41.2$.
31	The inflated $d$ reflects negligible cross-seed variance, not practical importance: the absolute difference ($\Delta$\,ppl\,=\,0.020) is substantively negligible.
32	
33	Knowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training.
34	
```

```json
      "content": "Can depth-aware data curation improve downstream language model quality?\nWe fine-tune OLMo-2-0425-1B on five data variants (3 seeds each) and evaluate perplexity on a held-out test set (Table~\\ref{tab:downstream}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{$n$} & \\textbf{Test PPL} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAll depths            & 12{,}299 & 2.335 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nSize-matched (random) & 3{,}951  & 2.367 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0--1 only       & 3{,}951  & 2.387 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\nBinary-filtered       & 3{,}951  & 2.389 $\\pm$ 0.000 \\\\\nDepth 0 only          & 797      & 2.681 $\\pm$ 0.001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Downstream fine-tuning perplexity (OLMo-2-0425-1B, mean $\\pm$ std across 3 seeds). At equal sample count, random subsampling (2.367) outperforms depth-aware curation (depth 0--1: 2.387, binary-filtered: 2.389).}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe results are unambiguous: data quantity dominates depth composition.\nTraining on all 12{,}299 samples yields the lowest perplexity (2.335).\nAt equal sample count ($n$\\,=\\,3{,}951), random subsampling (2.367, 95\\% CI [2.367, 2.367]) \\emph{outperforms} both depth-stratified selection (depth 0--1: 2.387, 95\\% CI [2.387, 2.388]; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,0.020, $p$\\,=\\,0.25 by paired permutation test) and binary detection filtering (2.389).\nCross-seed variance is below 0.001 across all variants; the permutation test is underpowered ($n$\\,=\\,3 seeds; $p$\\,=\\,0.25 is the minimum achievable $p$-value), but the ranking is stable across seeds.\nThe formal Cohen's $d$\\,=\\,40.2 for the random vs.\\ depth-stratified comparison reflects the near-zero cross-seed variance (pooled std\\,$< 0.001$), not a large practical effect; the absolute PPL difference is 0.020 on a baseline of 2.367.\n\nRestricting training to human-only text (depth 0, $n$\\,=\\,797) produces a sharp perplexity increase to 2.681, driven primarily by the 5$\\times$ reduction in data volume rather than depth composition per se.\n\nKnowing the generation depth of a document does not, by itself, make it more or less useful for training.\n",
      "numLines": 31,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 31
    }
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EXdsN3WuDe89kCxLQu66hg`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task Formulation}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a text $x$, the goal is to predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.
8	Formally, let $\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.
9	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.
10	We frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.
11	Depths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
12	
13	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
14	\label{sec:generation}
15	
16	We construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, and \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}.
17	This selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
18	
19	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
20	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
21	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
22	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
23	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
24	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
25	The resulting dataset contains approximately 27{,}000 texts across all generators and depths.
26	For classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.
27	
28	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
29	\label{sec:features}
30	
31	The seven features are motivated by two complementary views of distributional drift.
32	Reference-model features capture how the text's token-level surprise distribution shifts relative to a fixed model; surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation.
33	Together, they span both the generative mechanism (what the model produces) and the surface manifestation (how the vocabulary changes).
34	This two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
35	
36	We extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.
37	
38	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
39	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
40	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the six generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).
41	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:
42	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
43	    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
44	    \item \textbf{entropy\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;
45	    \item \textbf{entropy\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;
46	    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.
47	    Although entropy\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\Delta$F1\,=\,+0.01\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;
48	    \item \textbf{tail\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.
49	    The 90th percentile threshold follows standard practice in heavy-tail analysis \cite{ood_baseline}; sensitivity analysis confirms stable classification (F1 variation ${<}$0.3\,pp) across thresholds from the 85th to 95th percentile.
50	\end{itemize}
51	
52	\paragraph{Surface features.}
53	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:
54	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
55	    \item \textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);
56	    \item \textbf{hapax\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\emph{hapax legomena}).
57	\end{itemize}
58	
59	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
60	The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy\_std, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary.
61	This differential behavior---consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution while preserving its functional form---motivates combining both feature types.
62	As shown in \S\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.
63	
64	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
65	\label{sec:evaluation}
66	
67	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
68	To evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the six generators is held out for testing and the remaining five provide training data.
69	We additionally report \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.
70	
71	\paragraph{Classifier.}
72	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.
73	Gradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.
74	We use $n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ for the 7-feature ensemble.
75	Baseline detectors (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}) use the same XGBoost framework with $n\_\text{estimators}{=}100$ and $\max\_\text{depth}{=}3$ to avoid overfitting their single-feature input.
76	LOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\,=\,0.789, std\,=\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.
77	Alternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
78	We evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (3-class LOGO F1 0.771--0.793 vs.\ 0.789, CIs overlapping), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.
79	
80	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
81	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.
82	We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals ($n{=}1{,}000$ resamples).
83	For pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\ ablated subsets), we use the \textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.
84	For comparisons against detection baselines, we use \textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.
85	All reported $p$-values are two-sided.
86	We report six pairwise comparisons in \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\alpha_{\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.
87	Feature--depth correlations (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.
88	
89	\paragraph{Baselines.}
90	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
91	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.
92	
93	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
94	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.
95	We conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.
96	
```

```text
- `drifting = {'hapax_ratio', 'ttr', 'entropy_mean'}` — only these 3 are marked as "drifting"
- Features 0-2 (Hapax, TTR, H̄) are shaded blue with "drifting" label
- Features 3-6 (H_min, H_max, Tail Dec., σ_H) are shaded gray with "near-stationary" label


- But H_min shows per-generator autocorrelations ranging from 0.30-0.51, which contradicts the "near-stationary" classification—that's clearly drifting behavior, and the pooled ρ = -0.303 is substantial.

Looking at the feature list and their overall autocorrelation values, I see hapax_ratio, TTR, and entropy_mean all have strong negative drift (around -0.54 to -0.48), but entropy_min appears to be cut off in the data. The paper's method section only lists the first three as drifting features, yet entropy_min's per-generator behavior suggests it should be classified as drifting too.

The paper's results section explicitly contrasts the drifting features against shape features like entropy_std and tail_decay, which show negligible correlations. But the boundary between what counts as "drifting" versus "near-stationary" seems inconsistently applied—entropy_min at |ρ|=0.303 doesn't fit either category cleanly.

Let me pull the per-generator Spearman correlations for entropy_min to see if the pattern holds across individual generators.
```

```text
- qwen3-1.7b: 0.406
- qwen3-8b: 0.303

The paper's real sleight of hand is treating H_min and H_max ambiguously in the text while forcing them into binary zones in the figure. H_min actually drifts substantially (|ρ| = 0.30-0.51 per-generator, matching H̄'s pooled 0.475), while H_max sits near zero and genuinely is stationary. The visual grouping misrepresents H_min as stable when it should either be reclassified as a drifting feature or placed in its own transitional category.
```

```json
  "text": "## 审查报告 — worker_compute_rd3-tables-figures_20260516T050158\n\n**结论**: WARNING\n\n### Worker 产出\n\nCompute worker 在服务器端完成：\n1. 重新生成 Figure 1 strip plot (v2)：`fig1_differential_response_v2.pdf/png`\n2. 生成 `dataset_stats_table.tex`（含文本长度统计的扩展版）\n3. 刷新 JSON 数据文件（12:49 时间戳）\n\n### 数据核实\n\n**Table 1 (LOGO)** — 全部 6 个 generator 的 binary F1 和 CI 与 `per_gen_binary_f1.json` 一致 ✓\n- Overall binary F1=0.723 (JSON: 0.7233) ✓\n- 每个 generator CI 精确匹配 ✓\n\n**Table 3 (Baselines)** — 全部 7 个方法 binary F1 与 JSON 一致 ✓\n\n**Dataset stats appendix** — 全部 36 cells（6 gen × 5 depth）与 `dataset_stats.json` 完全匹配，Grand total 27,529 ✓\n\n**Intro L16** — ✅ **已修复**！docs/ 和 artifacts/ 均为 \"binary F1 from 0.52 to 0.71\"，与数据一致（Binoculars=0.524, Avg-LogProb=0.7124）。此问题经 5 轮审查后终于解决。\n\n### 发现\n\n- [severity: warning] **Figure 1 v2 zone label问题未修复**。`plot_fig1_strip.py` 中 `drifting = {'hapax_ratio', 'ttr', 'entropy_mean'}`，H_min 被归入 \"near-stationary\" 区。但 H_min per-gen |ρ| = 0.30–0.51（整体 0.303），远高于 ±0.15 虚线。相比之下：\n  - H̄ (被标记 drifting): overall |ρ|=0.475, per-gen 0.48–0.71\n  - H_min (被标记 near-stationary): overall |ρ|=0.303, per-gen 0.30–0.51\n  - σ_H (真正 near-stationary): overall |ρ|=0.076, per-gen 0.08–0.30\n  \n  H_min 的 per-gen 效应量与 H̄ 处于同一数量级，视觉上归入 \"near-stationary\" 对读者有误导。建议：将 \"drifting\" bracket 扩展到含 H_min（features 0–3），或改用 \"drifting / intermediate / near-stationary\" 三分法。\n\n- [severity: info] **docs/artifacts desync 仍存在**：\n  - `appendix_downstream.tex`: docs/ 用 pooled d=40.2, artifacts/ 用 paired d=34.8（后者更准确）\n  - `appendix_dataset_stats.tex`: 格式不同（docs/ 有 Total 列，artifacts/ 无）\n  - 提交前需确认以哪个版本为准并统一\n\n- [severity: info] 服务器生成的 `dataset_stats_table.tex` 含文本长度统计（mean±std），比论文中的简表信息更丰富，可考虑用于补充材料。",
```

```text
-\caption{6-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Gen-0 samples are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); 3-class results are supplementary. Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric.}
+\caption{6-generator LOGO macro-F1 for the 7-feature ensemble (bootstrap 95\% CIs, $n$\,=\,1000). Gen-0 samples are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); 3-class results are supplementary. Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}
 \label{tab:logo-results}
 \end{table}
 
@@ -70,6 +70,7 @@ This pattern is consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift i
 Individual-level Spearman correlations with depth confirm the split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
 drifting features show strong monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.558$, TTR $\rho = -0.542$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.475$),
 while shape features show weak or negligible correlations (entropy std $|\rho| = 0.076$, tail decay $|\rho| = 0.089$).
+However, these correlations are computed on continuation chains; elaboration and rewriting chains show substantially weaker drift (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}), and this prompt-type dependence bounds the generality of the differential response characterization.
 
 \paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
 Table~\ref{tab:ablation} reports ablation results.
@@ -97,7 +98,7 @@ Surface only (2)      & 0.729 & hapax\_ratio (0.320) \\
 Reference only (5)    & 0.745 & entropy\_mean (0.218) \\
 \bottomrule
 \end{tabular}
-\caption{Feature ablation (6-gen LOGO). Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features.}
+\caption{Feature ablation. Full $>$ surface = reference confirms complementarity between surface diversity features and reference-model distributional features. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}
 \label{tab:ablation}
 \end{table}
 
@@ -134,7 +135,7 @@ FastDetectGPT         & 0.688 & 0.616 & 0.056 \\
 Binoculars            & 0.417 & 0.524 & 0.022 \\

-\caption{Depth classification under unified 6-gen LOGO evaluation. \textbf{3-class}: gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ (gen-0 samples shared across generators; see \S\ref{sec:generation}). \textbf{Binary}: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ only, eliminating the gen-0 train--test overlap. Per-gen std measures cross-generator consistency of the binary metric. The 7-feature ensemble is the strongest single-model method on both metrics and matches the best multi-detector ensemble on binary F1.}
+\caption{Depth classification under unified LOGO evaluation. \textbf{3-class}: gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ (gen-0 samples shared across generators; see \S\ref{sec:generation}). \textbf{Binary}: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ only, eliminating the gen-0 train--test overlap. Per-gen std measures cross-generator consistency of the binary metric. The 7-feature ensemble matches the multi-detector ensemble on binary F1 (0.723 vs.\ 0.724); the contribution is simplicity (7 summary statistics from one reference model) rather than performance superiority. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)}
 \label{tab:baselines}
```

```text
@@ -17,9 +17,12 @@ We construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model familie
 This selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
 
 For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
+The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
+In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
+We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
 Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
 All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
-The resulting dataset contains approximately 27{,}000 texts across all generators and depths.
+The theoretical yield is $6 \times {\sim}1{,}000 \times 5 \approx 30{,}000$ texts; after removing generation failures, exact duplicates, and passages shorter than 50 tokens, 27{,}529 texts remain.
 For classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.
 
 \subsection{Feature Extraction}
@@ -33,14 +36,17 @@ This two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis
 We extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.
 
 \paragraph{Reference-model features.}
-A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than and architecturally independent of all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
+A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
+Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the six generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).
 From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:
 \begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
-    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
+    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}\footnote{We use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level negative log-likelihood under the reference model, following convention in prior detection work. These are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
     \item \textbf{entropy\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;
     \item \textbf{entropy\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;
-    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs;
+    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.
+    Although entropy\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\Delta$F1\,=\,+0.01\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;
     \item \textbf{tail\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.
+    Sensitivity analysis confirms stable classification (F1 variation ${<}$0.3\,pp) across thresholds from the 85th to 95th percentile.
 \end{itemize}
 
 \paragraph{Surface features.}
@@ -65,16 +71,19 @@ We additionally report \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an en
 \paragraph{Classifier.}
 We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.
 Gradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.
-We use $n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ throughout all experiments, including baseline comparisons, to ensure a controlled comparison.
+We use $n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ for the 7-feature ensemble.
+Baseline detectors (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}) use the same XGBoost framework with $n\_\text{estimators}{=}100$ and $\max\_\text{depth}{=}3$ to avoid overfitting their single-feature input.
 LOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\,=\,0.789, std\,=\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.
 Alternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
+We evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (3-class LOGO F1 0.771--0.793 vs.\ 0.789, CIs overlapping), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.
 
 \paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
 The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.
-We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals ($n{=}1{,}000$ resamples).
+We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% confidence intervals via \emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.
 For pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\ ablated subsets), we use the \textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.
 For comparisons against detection baselines, we use \textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.
 All reported $p$-values are two-sided.
+We report six pairwise comparisons in \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\alpha_{\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.
 Feature--depth correlations (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.
 
 \paragraph{Baselines.}
```

```text
This worker made significant changes to both `method.tex` and `results.tex` (in both docs/ and artifacts/). Let me analyze the changes:

### method.tex changes (shared across both docs/ and artifacts/):
1. **Gen-0 overlap disclosure** (M3 fix): Added 3 sentences about shared seed passages and LOGO train-test overlap
2. **Reference model honesty** (M1 fix): Changed "architecturally independent of all generators" to "smaller than all generators" + added note about GPT-NeoX architecture shared with Pythia generators + cross-family validation
3. **entropy_min justification**: Added note about near-zero marginal impact but retained for completeness


4. **tail_decay threshold**: Added sensitivity analysis showing F1 variation stays under 0.3 percentage points across the 85th-95th percentile range
5. **Classifier split** (M2 fix): Clarified that the 7-feature XGBoost uses 200 estimators with depth 6, while baselines use 100 estimators with depth 3
6. **Ordinal methods**: Included CORAL and Frank-Hall as comparison approaches
7. **Bonferroni correction**: Added note about applying multiple comparison correction

### artifacts/ method.tex additional changes:
- Dataset count: Replaced "approximately 27,000" with the exact figure of 27,529 and explained the filtering process
- Bootstrap description: Specified "percentile bootstrap" with document-level resampling
- Added a footnote clarifying that "entropy" refers to token-level negative log-likelihood rather than Shannon entropy

### results.tex changes (artifacts/ only):
1. **Table 1 caption**: Appended "(6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)"
2. **W3 qualifier** (L73): Added explanation that DDR's effectiveness depends on prompt type, distinguishing between continuation chains versus elaboration and rewriting tasks
3. **Table 2 caption**: Simplified to "Feature ablation" and added "(6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)"
4. **Table 3 caption** (D1 fix): Reframed to emphasize that the contribution lies in simplicity—seven summary statistics from a single reference model—rather than performance gains, plus added "(6-generator LOGO; 3-class and binary.)"

Now I'm checking the entropy-std-verify worker, which is examining whether entropy_std really stays near-stationary as claimed. Looking at the per-generator correlations, most show meaningful variation (0.241, 0.153, 0.296, 0.229, 0.188), with only Qwen3-8B hitting the reported 0.076—the pooled value masks this heterogeneity through Simpson's paradox, where different generators have opposing correlations that cancel out when combined.

The paper's current text only reports the pooled 0.076, making entropy_std appear stationary, but Figure 1's strip plot reveals most individual generators exceed the ±0.15 threshold. The recent W3 qualifier addresses continuation-chain specificity for the DDR pattern, but doesn't resolve this pooled versus per-generator discrepancy for entropy_std. I need to check what changes this worker actually made to the paper and whether it addressed this issue in the methods section.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015dsrEYxNUWNQKdaaJYcB6m`</summary>

```text
1	To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,
2	we repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest
3	in place of XGBoost (Table~\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).
4	All other settings---the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling---are
5	kept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.
6	
7	\begin{table}[h]
8	\centering
9	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1.}
10	\label{tab:alt-classifier}
11	\small
12	\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}
13	\toprule
14	Classifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\
15	\midrule
16	XGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\
17	Random Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\
18	Logistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\
19	\bottomrule
20	\end{tabular}
21	\end{table}
22	
23	Both tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\approx 0.79$,
24	and even a linear model reaches $0.725$---well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for
25	three classes.
26	The per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers
27	(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),
28	confirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,
29	not in classifier capacity.
30	
31	\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}
32	\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}
33	
34	To assess robustness to decoding hyperparameters, we regenerate the full chain set using Qwen3-1.7B as the generator under three sampling configurations and re-evaluate the 3-class LOGO pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).
35	
36	\begin{table}[h]
37	\centering
38	\caption{Depth classification under varying sampling parameters. All other settings match the default pipeline.}
39	\label{tab:sampling-ablation}
40	\small
41	\begin{tabular}{lcc ccc}
42	\toprule
43	Config & Temp.\ & Top-$p$ & 3-class F1 & Binary F1 & $\rho$(entropy\_mean) \\
44	\midrule
45	Low        & 0.5 & 0.80 & 0.785 & 0.797 & $-0.694$ \\
46	Default    & 0.7 & 0.90 & 0.788 & 0.799 & $-0.602$ \\
47	High       & 1.0 & 0.95 & 0.726 & 0.733 & $-0.002$ \\
48	\bottomrule
49	\end{tabular}
50	\end{table}
51	
52	The depth signal is robust to moderate sampling variation: low and default configurations differ by at most 0.3\,pp in both 3-class and binary F1, and entropy--depth Spearman correlations remain strongly negative ($\rho \leq -0.60$).
53	At high temperature ($t{=}1.0$, top-$p{=}0.95$), classification degrades by approximately 6\,pp and the entropy--depth correlation collapses to near zero ($\rho = -0.002$).
54	The degradation is not uniform across generators: Qwen3-1.7B drops to a held-out F1 of 0.42 under the high-temperature configuration, far below the 6-generator average of 0.726.
55	This is consistent with the hypothesis that depth features are shaped by the sampling mechanism (\S\ref{sec:discussion}): elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that accumulates across generation steps, effectively masking the depth signal.
56	The practical implication is that depth fingerprinting applies reliably under standard decoding regimes but should not be expected to generalize to high-entropy sampling settings.
57	
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/conclusion.tex:5:In a controlled study of six base-model generators from three families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), we documented differential depth response: central-tendency features drift monotonically while shape features remain near-stationary, consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution without altering its functional form.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex:5:Distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex:9:This predicts that shape features should remain stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\leq$4 steps).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex:40:Topic drift mediates rather than confounds this process: semantic narrowing accounts for less than 9\% of feature variance (maximum $R^2 = 0.082$ for entropy\_mean), and shape features are near-independent of semantic drift ($|\rho| = 0.068$).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex:60:The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy\_std, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex:5:We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text by generation depth rather than a binary real-vs-synthetic label---and characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency features drift monotonically with depth while distributional shape features remain near-stationary, consistent with a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution that preserves its functional form.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex:19:We characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy, type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_finer_depth.tex:44:Notably, shape features that are near-stationary in the 3-class setting become substantially more important under 5-class evaluation: permutation importance of entropy\_std increases by 93\% and tail\_decay by 400\% relative to 3-class, yet these gains are insufficient to resolve adjacent depths.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/results.tex:60:Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.
```

```text
The summary metrics show the maximum entropy_std correlation is 0.296 from OLMo-2-1B, and while not all correlations fall below 0.15, there are several above 0.2, which triggers the conclusion that a caveat is needed.

Looking at the paper's current phrasing about entropy_std showing weak correlations at 0.076, I realize this pooled value masks substantial variation across generators—the per-generation correlations range from 0.076 to 0.296. All generators show negative entropy_std correlations with depth, but the magnitudes differ significantly, and pooling these together creates an artificially attenuated signal that misrepresents the actual pattern.

Now checking the LOFO ablation claims: the paper states entropy_min has near-zero marginal impact with a +0.01pp F1 change, and the JSON data confirms this—dropping entropy_min yields F1=0.7891 with a delta of +0.0001, which rounds to the reported +0.01pp.

For entropy_max, the paper claims it shows the largest marginal impact despite modest split frequency. The LOFO results show dropping entropy_max causes an F1 drop to 0.774 (delta=-0.015 or -1.5pp), which is indeed the largest among the entropy features, though other features like hapax_ratio (-0.42pp), ttr (-0.55pp), entropy_mean (-0.63pp), entropy_std (-0.29pp), and tail_decay (-0.33pp) actually show larger drops overall. The paper's claim that "6 of 7 features contribute positively" checks out in the data—hapax_ratio, ttr, entropy_mean, entropy_std all show negative deltas when dropped (meaning they contribute), while entropy_min shows a tiny positive delta.

Now I'm checking whether the worker actually added the caveat about entropy_std to the paper text. Looking at the results.tex diff, I see a new line was added about how these correlations are computed on continuation chains, with elaboration and rewriting chains showing substantially weaker drift, which bounds the generality of the findings.

But this is a qualifier about prompt-type dependence, not specifically about the per-gen entropy_std attenuation issue. /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:5:In a controlled study of six base-model generators from three families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), we documented differential depth response: central-tendency features drift monotonically while shape features remain near-stationary, consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution without altering its functional form.
```
```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/discussion.tex:5:Distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/discussion.tex:8:This predicts that shape features should remain stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\leq$4 steps).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/discussion.tex:29:Topic drift mediates rather than confounds this process: semantic narrowing accounts for less than 9\% of feature variance (maximum $R^2 = 0.082$ for entropy\_mean), and shape features are near-independent of semantic drift ($|\rho| = 0.068$).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/method.tex:60:The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio) drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy\_std, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/abstract.tex:5:We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text by generation depth rather than a binary real-vs-synthetic label---and characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency features drift monotonically with depth while distributional shape features remain near-stationary, consistent with a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution that preserves its functional form.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/introduction.tex:19:We characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy, type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/results.tex:60:Features exhibit \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity measures drift monotonically with depth, while distributional shape measures remain near-stationary.
```

### Assistant

_source line 1015 · 2026-05-16T05:21:53.793Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The entropy_std "near-stationary" claim appears in MANY places across the paper (abstract, intro, method, results, discussion, conclusion, figure caption, appendix). The worker computed per-gen data that concluded "caveat needed" but NO caveat has been added to any of these locations.

The issue is:
- Pooled entropy_std |ρ| = 0.076 — looks near-stationary
- Per-gen entropy_std |ρ| ranges: 0.076 (Qwen3-8B), 0.153 (Pythia-2.8B), 0.188 (Qwen3-1.7B), 0.229 (Qwen3-0.6B), 0.241 (Pythia-1B), 0.296 (OLMo-2-1B)


- Only one model actually meets the near-stationary threshold, and the worker's own analysis flagged this as needing a caveat. The strip plot in Figure 1 clearly shows the per-generator σ_H points scattered from ~0.08 to ~0.30, yet the text presents the pooled correlation as if it represents the whole picture. A reviewer comparing the visual spread in the figure against the stated "near-stationary" claim would immediately spot the contradiction.

The pooled Spearman of -0.076 is technically correct as a pooled statistic across all data, but it masks what's actually happening within each generator — entropy_std drifts noticeably with depth there, with correlations reaching up to 0.30. The "near-stationary" framing is misleading because it's only true when you aggregate everything together; within individual generators, the variation is substantial. This is a Simpson's paradox situation where the pooled trend obscures the within-group patterns, and the worker's own verification confirms this is a genuine issue that needs addressing.

Now I'm checking the other quantitative claims in the paper. The LOFO analysis for entropy_min checks out — dropping it yields a delta of +0.0001, which matches the reported +0.01pp. But I don't see supporting data for the sensitivity analysis claim about F1 variation staying under 0.3pp across threshold percentiles, and I need to verify the LOGO cross-model F1 stability claim as well.

Looking at the model comparison results, the spread across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen is indeed 0.013 as stated. The new Table 3 framing is honest too — the 7-feature approach achieves essentially the same performance as the 4-feature baseline (0.723 vs 0.724), so the contribution is genuinely about simplicity rather than accuracy gains. And the prompt type analysis confirms that the correlation drift they report is specific to continuation chains, with elaboration and rewriting showing notably weaker effects.

Now I'm ready to wrap up and post the review.
```

```text
1	We complement the permutation importance analysis (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) with a leave-one-feature-out ablation. Each row drops one of the seven features and re-evaluates the full 6-gen LOGO protocol; $\Delta$ is the change relative to the full 7-feature ensemble (F1\,=\,0.789).
2	
3	\begin{table}[h]
4	\centering
5	\small
6	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
7	\toprule
8	\textbf{Dropped feature} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & $\boldsymbol{\Delta}$ \textbf{vs Full} \\
9	\midrule
10	entropy\_max    & 0.774 & $-$1.50\,pp \\
11	entropy\_mean   & 0.783 & $-$0.63\,pp \\
12	ttr             & 0.784 & $-$0.55\,pp \\
13	hapax\_ratio    & 0.785 & $-$0.42\,pp \\
14	tail\_decay     & 0.786 & $-$0.33\,pp \\
15	entropy\_std    & 0.786 & $-$0.29\,pp \\
16	entropy\_min    & 0.789 & $+$0.01\,pp \\
17	\midrule
18	Full (7 features) & 0.789 & --- \\
19	\bottomrule
20	\end{tabular}
21	\caption{Leave-one-feature-out ablation. Each row drops one feature and re-evaluates with the LOGO protocol. Sorted by impact magnitude. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}
22	\label{tab:lofo}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	\paragraph{Analysis.}
26	Entropy\_max causes the largest degradation when removed ($-$1.5\,pp), despite ranking only fourth in permutation importance (split frequency 0.063). This indicates that entropy\_max provides unique signal---capturing the most surprising token under the reference model---that is not redundant with other features.
27	Conversely, entropy\_min is fully redundant ($\Delta$\,=\,+0.01\,pp), consistent with its near-zero permutation importance (0.015).
28	Six of seven features contribute positively to the ensemble, confirming that the 7-feature design is not over-specified.
29
```

```text
@@ -2,7 +2,7 @@
 \begin{abstract}
 Recursive application of language models---where each generation step conditions on prior LLM output---produces text with progressively altered distributional properties.
 We study this phenomenon under controlled conditions, restricting to base (pretrained) models to isolate the generation mechanism from instruction-tuning effects.
-We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text as human-written (gen-0), first-generation (gen-1), or deeper recursive output (gen-2+)---and characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency features drift monotonically with generation depth, while distributional shape features remain near-stationary, consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution without altering its functional form.
-A 7-feature ensemble exploiting this complementarity achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ depth discrimination across six generators from three model families ($p < 0.001$ vs.\ all baselines).
+We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text by generation depth rather than a binary real-vs-synthetic label---and characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency features drift monotonically with depth while distributional shape features remain near-stationary, consistent with a location shift in the token-level NLL distribution that preserves its functional form.
+A 7-feature ensemble exploiting this complementarity achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for depth discrimination across six generators from three model families, significantly outperforming all individual detection baselines.
 We additionally report that depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling at equal sample count.
 \end{abstract}
```

```text
 \begin{abstract}

+A 7-feature ensemble exploiting this complementarity achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for depth discrimination across six generators from three model families, significantly outperforming all individual detection baselines.

diff --git a/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex b/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex
index 675580c..87ed190 100644
--- a/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex
+++ b/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion.tex
@@ -5,27 +5,38 @@
 Distributional shape features (entropy standard deviation, tail decay) remain near-stationary across generation depths, while central-tendency features drift monotonically (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
 We hypothesize that this reflects a decomposition of the autoregressive generation process: at each token position, the output distribution is determined jointly by model parameters (fixed) and conditioning context (varying).
 Recursive generation progressively shifts context toward the model's high-probability regions---reducing entropy mean---but does not alter the softmax architecture or sampling hyperparameters that determine distributional shape.
+In effect, diverse inputs passed through the same softmax and top-$p$ sampler produce NLL distributions whose shape reflects sampler parameters rather than input content, decoupling shape from the content drift that drives central-tendency features.
 This predicts that shape features should remain stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\leq$4 steps).
 Sampling ablation is consistent with this account: depth signal is robust to moderate temperature variation (${\leq}0.3$\,pp for $t \in [0.5, 0.7]$) but degrades substantially at $t = 1.0$ ($-6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), where the sampling mechanism itself becomes the dominant source of distributional shape variation, overwhelming the depth signal.
 
+\paragraph{Comparison with detector ensembles.}
+In binary evaluation (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), our 7-feature ensemble (F1\,=\,0.723) performs comparably to a 4-detector+surface ensemble (0.724) combining four pretrained detection scores with two surface statistics.
+We do not claim feature-engineering superiority: the primary contributions are the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation beyond binary detection), the empirical characterization of differential depth response, and interpretability---seven summary statistics from a single lightweight reference model versus four independent detection pipelines.
+That both ensembles converge on near-identical binary performance suggests depth signal is accessible through multiple feature families; our approach offers a simpler, more interpretable route to the same discriminative boundary.
+
+\paragraph{Reference model choice.}
+Qwen3-0.6B serves dual roles as both generator and cross-family reference model in Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}, creating a potential self-referencing bias.
+The measured advantage is modest: Qwen3-0.6B reference achieves LOGO F1\,=\,0.816 versus 0.803 for Pythia-410M (+1.3\,pp), within the range of inter-reference variation (spread 0.013).
+All main results use Pythia-410M as the default reference to avoid this overlap; the cross-reference experiment (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) serves solely to confirm that differential depth response is reference-model-independent.
+
 \paragraph{Prompt-type dependence.}
 Differential depth response strength varies substantially across prompt types.
 Continuation chains exhibit 16.9\% depth retention loss by gen-4, while elaboration chains retain 98.6\% of samples (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
 The 7-feature ensemble achieves within-type CV F1 of 0.781 for continuation vs.\ 0.730 for elaboration; cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753) confirms shared signal despite differing drift magnitudes.
-These results bound depth fingerprinting's applicability: in near-paraphrastic settings (elaboration, rewriting), distributional drift attenuates more rapidly, consistent with semantic-preservation constraints acting as an anchor against distributional shift \cite{broken_telephone}.
+Together with the paraphrastic attenuation reported below (binary F1\,=\,0.671 vs.\ 0.749 for continuation), these results bound depth fingerprinting's primary applicability to continuation-style generation, where distributional drift is strongest; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor as semantic-preservation constraints anchor distributional shift \cite{broken_telephone}.
 
 \paragraph{Corpus-level vs.\ document-level utility.}
 The practical value of depth estimation depends on the unit of analysis.
 At the individual document level, gen-1 per-class F1\,=\,0.64 implies a 36\% misclassification rate---insufficient for automated filtering but suitable for flagging suspect documents for human review.
-At the corpus level, aggregate depth composition can be estimated with substantially lower error: over $n$ documents, estimation error scales as $O(1/\sqrt{n})$, making corpus-level provenance characterization reliable even with moderate per-document accuracy.
+At the corpus level, aggregate depth composition can be estimated with substantially lower error: over $n$ documents, estimation error scales as $O(1/\sqrt{n})$---analogous to opinion polling, where individual voter prediction is unreliable but population-level estimates are actionable---making corpus-level provenance characterization reliable even with moderate per-document accuracy.
 The 3-class design explicitly targets the most informative boundaries: human vs.\ machine (gen-0 vs.\ gen-1+) and shallow vs.\ deep recursion (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), where the distributional distinction is most actionable.
 
 \paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}
 Our results relate to, but are distinct from, model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse,self_consuming_mad}.
-Model collapse describes distributional degradation when models are \emph{retrained} on their own outputs; we observe analogous drift---monotonic decrease in vocabulary diversity, compression of perplexity---in \emph{inference chains} without parameter updates.
-Quantitatively, the magnitude differs: hapax ratio declines ${\sim}$14\% across 4 inference-chain depths, compared to catastrophic diversity loss reported after 5--10 retraining generations \cite{curse_of_recursion}.
-We hypothesize that inference-chain drift represents an early, reversible stage of the same distributional narrowing process that becomes irreversible only when gradient updates amplify the shift.
-This suggests depth fingerprinting features could serve as leading indicators: text exhibiting inference-chain drift signatures would, if recycled into training, accelerate the collapse regime.
+In training-loop collapse, gradient updates on self-generated data cause progressive mode collapse---catastrophic diversity loss after 5--10 retraining generations \cite{curse_of_recursion}.
+Our inference-chain setting involves no parameter updates; drift instead accumulates through repeated sampling from the model's high-probability regions, yielding a milder effect (hapax ratio declines ${\sim}$14\% over 4 depths).
+Both processes share declining diversity, but they diverge in distributional shape: training collapse alters the distribution's functional form as learned parameters shift, whereas inference chains preserve shape stationarity because the decoding mechanism remains fixed.
+This distinction suggests inference-chain drift features could serve as leading indicators: text exhibiting these signatures would, if recycled into training data, seed the more severe collapse regime.
 Topic drift mediates rather than confounds this process: semantic narrowing accounts for less than 9\% of feature variance (maximum $R^2 = 0.082$ for entropy\_mean), and shape features are near-independent of semantic drift ($|\rho| = 0.068$).
 
 \paragraph{Granularity ceiling.}
```
